Since there are custom network ACLs configured, you need to explicitly configure ephemeral ports for outbound traffic.
    
    The option that says: **Creating a new rule on the default network ACL to allow outbound traffic on ephemeral ports 1024 – 65535** is incorrect because default network ACLs already allow traffic on all ports by default and the issue is still happening. You need to configure the custom network ACLs used by the subnets.
    
- Create a new rule on the custom network ACL to allow outbound traffic on ports 80 and 443.
- Creating a new rule on the default network ACL to allow outbound traffic on ephemeral ports 1024 – 65535.

A global technology company has a cloud architecture that uses various VPCs across multiple regions. To monitor their entire system, you were instructed to aggregate the CPU Utilization of their Reserved EC2 instances running in all of their VPCs.

How can you implement this requirement in the easiest way possible with minimal additional costs? (Select TWO.)

- **Set up a CloudWatch dashboard. Add a widget and choose Math expression under Graph Metric to query and aggregate the CPU Utilization of all Reserved EC2 instances in all regions.**
- Set up a data dashboard in AWS QuickSight. Add a widget for each region that contains the aggregated CPU utilization for all EC2 instances that are running on a specific region.
- **Enable detailed monitoring for all EC2 instances.**
- Use the AWS Service Health Dashboard to check the aggregated CPU Utilization of all of your EC2 instances in all regions.
- Enable basic monitoring for all EC2 instances.
- Set up a CloudWatch dashboard. Add a widget and select Cross-Region under Graph Metric to aggregate the CPU Utilization of all Reserved EC2 instances in all regions.
- **Evaluations**
    
    You can aggregate the metrics for AWS resources across multiple accounts and Regions. For example, you can aggregate statistics for your EC2 instances that have detailed monitoring enabled. Instances that use basic monitoring are not included. Therefore, you must enable detailed monitoring (at an additional charge), which provides data in 1-minute periods.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/AWS-EC2-DetailedMonitoring.PNG)
    
    AWS allows you to use CloudWatch metric math to aggregate and transform metrics from multiple accounts and Regions. Metric math enables you to query multiple CloudWatch metrics and use math expressions to create new time series based on these metrics. You can visualize the resulting time series on the CloudWatch console and add them to dashboards. You can add a math expression to a graph on your CloudWatch dashboard.
    
    Therefore, the correct answers are the options that say:
    
    **– Set up a CloudWatch dashboard. Add a widget and choose Math expression under Graph Metric to query and aggregate the CPU Utilization of all Reserved EC2 instances in all regions.**
    
    **– Enable detailed monitoring for all EC2 instances.**
    
    The option that says: **Set up a CloudWatch dashboard. Add a widget and select Cross-Region under Graph Metric to aggregate the CPU Utilization of all Reserved EC2 instances in all regions** is incorrect because there is no option to select Cross-Region under Graph Metric. Although cross-region graphing is supported, you need to use Metric math to aggregate cross-region metrics.
    
    The option that says: **Set up a data dashboard in AWS QuickSight then add a widget for each region that contains the aggregated CPU utilization for all EC2 instances that are running on a specific region** is incorrect because a data dashboard in AWS QuickSight is mainly used for business intelligence and not to monitor your AWS resources.
    
    **Using the AWS Service Health Dashboard to check the aggregated CPU Utilization of all of your EC2 instances in all regions** is incorrect because the AWS Service Health Dashboard is mainly used to monitor the entire service health of AWS globally and not just your account.
    
    **Enabling basic monitoring for all EC2 instances** is incorrect because you need to enable detailed monitoring and not just basic monitoring.
    

AWS regularly releases service updates and changes to constantly improve its entire global architecture that might affect your cloud resources. To ensure the continuous flow of your business, you need to be immediately notified for any outages or any underlying infrastructure changes that AWS has made.

Which of the following services can help you in this scenario?

- AWS Trusted Advisor
- **AWS Health Dashboard**
    
    The **AWS Health Dashboard** is the single place to learn about the availability and operations of AWS services. You can view the overall status of AWS services, and you can sign in to view personalized communications about your particular AWS account or organization. Your account view provides deeper visibility into resource issues, upcoming changes, and important notifications.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/AWS-Health-Dashboard.JPG)
    
    Upon logging in to the AWS Health Dashboard, you can have a customized overview of the AWS service status that your application relies on. The dashboard can be used to gain insight into particular operational incidents that impact your AWS account. As an instance, if you encounter an event regarding a missing Amazon Elastic Block Store (EBS) volume that is linked to an Amazon EC2 instance, you can swiftly assess the extent of the impact on your resources. This, in turn, can assist in the identification and resolution of the issue.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **AWS Health Dashboard.**
    
    **AWS Trusted Advisor** is incorrect because it is an online resource which helps you reduce cost, increase performance, and improve security by optimizing your AWS environment, and it also provides real time guidance to help you provision your resources while following AWS best practices. It does not, however, tell you if there are events that occur with AWS that will affect your business.
    
    **AWS IAM** is incorrect since it is a web service that helps you securely control access to AWS resources. You use IAM to control who is authenticated and authorized to use resources. IAM does not tell you if something occurs to AWS that will impact your business.
    
    **AWS Billing Dashboard** is incorrect since it is used to monitor your AWS usage, and budget your costs. It shows all relevant information regarding your billing in AWS, and does not provide alerts and remediation guidance when AWS is experiencing events that may impact you.
    
- AWS IAM
- AWS Billing Dashboard

A leading media company is utilizing a wide range of instance store-backed and EBS-backed EC2 instances in their VPC. There are a couple of instances that always go from the `pending` state to the `terminated` state immediately after restarting it.

Which of the following is a possible cause of this problem? (Select TWO.)

- Each instance has an Elastic Fabric Adapter (EFA) attached.
- **You've reached your EBS volume limit.**
- **The instance store-backed AMI that you used to launch the instance is missing a required part.**
- You have reached the limit on the number of instances that you can launch in a region.
- AWS does not currently have enough available On-Demand capacity to service your request.
- **Evaluation**
    
    If your EC2 instance goes from the `pending`state to the `terminated` state immediately after restarting then it could be caused by one of the following reasons:
    
    - You’ve reached your EBS volume limit.
    - An EBS snapshot is corrupt.
    - The root EBS volume is encrypted and you do not have permissions to access the KMS key for decryption.
    - The instance store-backed AMI that you used to launch the instance is missing a required part (an image.part.xx file).
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/aws_troubleshooting_terminated_ebs.PNG)
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– You’ve reached your EBS volume limit.**
    
    **– The instance store-backed AMI that you used to launch the instance is missing a required part.**
    
    The option that says: **Each instance has an Elastic Fabric Adapter (EFA) attached** is incorrect because an Elastic Fabric Adapter (EFA) is simply a network interface for Amazon EC2 instances that enables customers to run applications requiring high levels of inter-node communications at scale on AWS. Having an EFA attached to the instance won’t result in this error.
    
    The option that says: **You have reached the limit on the number of instances that you can launch in a region** is incorrect because this option describes the root cause for getting an `InstanceLimitExceeded` error.
    
    The option that says: **AWS does not currently have enough available On-Demand capacity to service your request** is incorrect because this is the root cause when you get the InsufficientInstanceCapacity error when you try to launch a new instance or restart a stopped instance, but it does not cause the instance to be in the `terminated` state immediately after restarting it.
    

The development team of a software company has just released the next version of their application in production. It is hosted in an Amazon ECS Cluster with RDS as its database and uses Route 53 as its DNS service. The new version of the application has undergone testing and now needs to be promoted to production from a separate environment. In order to have a smooth transition and avoid any unwanted outages, you need an initial set of traffic to be directed to the new version of the application for testing before doing the final cutover.

Which of the following will you implement on Route 53 to achieve this?

- 2 resource records based on Simple Routing policy
- 1 resource record based on Latency Routing policy
- 1 resource record based on Geolocation Routing policy
- **2 resource records based on Weighted Routing policy**
    
    **Amazon Route 53** provides highly available and scalable Domain Name System (DNS), domain name registration, and health-checking web services. It is designed to give developers and businesses an extremely reliable and cost effective way to route end users to Internet applications by translating names like example.com into the numeric IP addresses, such as 192.0.2.1, that computers use to connect to each other.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/AWS-Route53-Weighted.PNG)
    
    Route 53 weighted routing policy lets you direct traffic to multiple resources in proportions that you specify. It lets you associate multiple resources with a single domain name (tutorialsdojo.com) or a subdomain (portal.tutorialsdojo.com) and choose how much traffic is routed to each resource. This can be useful for a variety of purposes, including load balancing and testing new versions of software.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **2 resource records based on Weighted Routing policy.**
    
    The option that says: **2 resource records based on Simple Routing policy** is incorrect since simple routing policy is used to configure standard DNS records. If you specify multiple values on a simple routing policy, the resolver will return values in a random order.
    
    The option that says: **1 resource record based on Latency Routing policy** is incorrect since latency routing is used for resources in multiple AWS regions and you want to route traffic to the region that provides the best latency.
    
    The option that says: **1 resource record based on Geolocation Routing policy** is incorrect since geolocation routing is used if you want to route traffic to a specific region based on the location of your users.
    

A data analytics firm has opened a new service center in Africa, and it must be operational in a few days. The new service center decided to contact Company HQ to request a list of running AWS resources and configurations being used in other service centers. The priority is to create a CloudFormation template that includes initial resources and configurations crucial for operations. Remaining templates for less critical resources will follow later.

Which among the choices will you send out first?

- **CloudFormation design that includes EC2 servers, Redshift, and EMR configurations.**
    
    Given that the company is a data analytics firm, they should first be provided with the necessary tools that would enable them to perform data analytics at some velocity, such as RedShift and EMR.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/2018-11-05_22-34-41-79e5dc33553519a259497d0d5b9becf1.png)
    
    Hence, the correct answer is:
    
    **CloudFormation design that includes EC2 servers, Redshift, and EMR configurations.**
    
    The option that says: **CloudFormation design that includes EC2 servers, EBS, AWS Glue protocols, and ElastiCache configurations** is incorrect because AWS Glue is primarily used for ETL (Extract, Transform, Load) processes to prepare data for analysis. However, without the foundational data infrastructure like a data warehouse (Redshift) or big data processing service (EMR) set up first, there’s no data to process. ElastiCache is primarily for caching and fast data retrieval, which isn’t the main focus at this stage.
    
    The option that says: **CloudFormation design that includes EC2 servers, EBS, CloudFront settings, and S3 configurations** is incorrect because CloudFront is a CDN service that is mainly used for high content delivery availability and performance. S3, meanwhile, is used for object storage.
    
    The option that says: **CloudFormation design that includes EC2 servers, CloudWatch custom metrics, and SQS configurations** is incorrect because CloudWatch is for monitoring your resources, and SQS is for queueing messages needed by your resources.
    
- CloudFormation design that includes EC2 servers, CloudWatch custom metrics, and SQS configurations.
- CloudFormation design that includes EC2 servers, EBS, CloudFront settings, and S3 configurations.
- CloudFormation design that includes EC2 servers, EBS, AWS Glue protocols, and ElastiCache configurations.

A startup launched an online transaction processing application that uses a fleet of EC2 instances deployed across multiple Availability Zones with an Application Load Balancer to evenly distribute the incoming traffic. The SysOps Administrator provisioned an RDS instance in a private subnet for the application’s database-tier, with a NAT gateway for routing traffic from the database server to the Internet.

Which of the following options can the Administrator implement to increase the scalability and availability of the application? (Select TWO.)

- Switch from a NAT gateway to NAT instance for higher bandwidth and availability
- **Configure the RDS instance to use Multi-AZ deployments**
- Replace the Application Load Balancer with Route 53
- **Use Auto Scaling**
- **Evaluation**
    
    **Amazon EC2 Auto Scaling** helps you ensure that you have the correct number of Amazon EC2 instances available to handle the load for your application. You create collections of EC2 instances called Auto Scaling groups. You can specify the minimum number of instances in each Auto Scaling group, and Amazon EC2 Auto Scaling ensures that your group never goes below this size. You can specify the maximum number of instances in each Auto Scaling group, and Amazon EC2 Auto Scaling ensures that your group never goes above this size.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/con-multi-AZ.png)
    
    Amazon RDS Multi-AZ deployments provide enhanced availability and durability for Database (DB) Instances, making them a natural fit for production database workloads. When you provision a Multi-AZ DB Instance, Amazon RDS automatically creates a primary DB Instance and synchronously replicates the data to a standby instance in a different Availability Zone (AZ).
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Use Auto Scaling.**
    
    **– Configure the RDS instance to use Multi-AZ deployments.**
    
    The option that says: **Switch from a NAT gateway to NAT instance for higher bandwidth and availability** is incorrect because a NAT gateway is actually much better than a NAT instance in terms of scalability and availability.
    
    The option that says: **Replace the Application Load Balancer with Route 53** is incorrect because an application load balancer provides better performance, compared to Route53, in evenly distributing the incoming traffic to the EC2 instances.
    

A web application is running on Amazon EC2 instances behind an Application Load Balancer (ALB) within an Auto Scaling group. However, log inspection revealed that the application is being targeted by malicious traffic originating from a single public IP address. The SysOps administrator must implement a solution that will block this IP address.

Which solution must the SysOps administrator implement to meet this requirement?

- Utilize AWS Resource Access Manager (AWS RAM) to administer traffic rules and block malicious activity from the internet. Associate the ALB with AWS RAM.
- Utilize Amazon Detective to prevent malicious activity from the internet and monitor the traffic. Associate the ALB with Amazon Detective.
- **Create an IP set that includes the public IP address. Configure a web ACL with a rule set to BLOCK the IP set in AWS WAF. Associate the ALB with the web ACL.**
    
    AWS WAF is a web application firewall that lets you monitor the HTTP(S) requests that are forwarded to your protected web application resources. You can protect the following resource types:
    
    – Amazon CloudFront distribution
    
    – Amazon API Gateway REST API
    
    – Application Load Balancer
    
    – AWS AppSync GraphQL API
    
    – Amazon Cognito user pool
    
    – AWS App Runner service
    
    – AWS Verified Access instance
    
    AWS WAF lets you control access to your content. Based on criteria that you specify, such as the IP addresses that requests originate from or the values of query strings, the service associated with your protected resource responds to requests either with the requested content, with an HTTP 403 status code (Forbidden), or with a custom response.
    
    A web access control list (web ACL) gives you fine-grained control over all of the HTTP(S) web requests that your protected resource responds to.
    
    To create a new web ACL, use the web ACL creation wizard.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/Web_ACL_AWS_WAF_block_IP_address_BJL_16112023.png)
    
    You can use criteria like the following to allow or block requests:
    
    – IP address origin of the request
    
    – Country of origin of the request
    
    – String match or regular expression (regex) match in a part of the request
    
    – Size of a particular part of the request
    
    - Detection of malicious SQL code or scripting
    
    You can utilize web ACL to allow or block requests from a specific IP address. You can create an IP set that will include the IP address and use a rule that will block the IP set when configuring a web ACL.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Create an IP set that includes the public IP address. Configure a web ACL with a rule set to BLOCK the IP set in AWS WAF. Associate the ALB with the web ACL.**
    
    The option that says: **Utilize AWS Resource Access Manager (AWS RAM) to administer traffic rules and block malicious activity from the internet. Associate the ALB with AWS RAM** is incorrect because AWS RAM is primarily used to securely share your resources across AWS accounts, within your organization or organizational units (OUs), and with AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) roles and users for supported resource types, it is not for blocking traffic from a specific IP address. The AWS RAM only supports a limited number of resource types, and resources for an Application Load Balancer is not supported. It is an entirely different service and is impossible to utilize for this approach.
    
    The option that says: **Utilize Amazon Detective to prevent malicious activity from the internet and monitor the traffic. Associate the ALB with Amazon Detective** is incorrect. Although Amazon Detective can help investigate and analyze IP traffic to identify malicious activities, it is still inadequate as Amazon Detective is not capable of blocking requests from a specific IP address. With Amazon Detective, you can analyze and visualize security data to investigate potential security issues. Detective collects and analyzes events that describe IP traffic, AWS management operations, and malicious or unauthorized activity from AWS CloudTrail logs, VPC Flow Logs, Amazon GuardDuty findings, and, Amazon EKS audit logs.
    
    The option that says: **Create an NACL rule to deny all inbound traffic from the public IP address. Associate the ALB with the NACL** is incorrect. Network Access Control Lists (NACLs) can only be associated with subnets, not directly with individual resources like an ALB. Moreover, managing IP blocklists with NACLs can be less flexible compared to AWS WAF.
    
- Create an NACL rule to deny all inbound traffic from the public IP address. Associate the ALB with the NACL.

A large IT solutions company with 200 technical personnel uses AWS Organizations to manage its multi-account AWS environment. All AWS accounts should be able to read a specific Amazon S3 bucket in the management account. Based on the company policy, the S3 bucket should not be accessible outside the organization. The SysOps administrator needs to create a bucket policy to create the necessary permissions.

Which parameters should be specified on the bucket policy to accomplish this task in the MOST efficient approach?

- Manually list all AWS member accounts as the principal.
- **Declare a `(*)` wildcard as the principal and set `PrincipalOrgId` as a condition.**
    
    To grant permission to everyone, also referred as anonymous access, you set the wildcard (“*”) as the Principal value. For example, if you configure your bucket as a website, you want all the objects in the bucket to be publicly accessible. The following are equivalent.
    
    – `"Principal":"*"`
    
    – `"Principal":{"AWS":"*"}`
    
    **aws:PrincipalOrgID**
    
    Use this key to compare the identifier of the organization in AWS Organizations to which the requesting principal belongs with the identifier specified in the policy.
    
    Availability – This key is included in the request context only if the principal is a member of an organization.
    
    This global key provides an alternative to listing all the account IDs for all AWS accounts in an organization. You can use this condition key to simplify specifying the Principal element in a resource-based policy. You can specify the organization ID in the condition element. When you add and remove accounts, policies that include the aws:PrincipalOrgID key automatically include the correct accounts and don’t require manual updating.
    
    For example, the following Amazon S3 bucket policy allows members of any account in the o-xxxxxxxxxxx organization to add an object into the policy-ninja-dev bucket.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/aws_iam_principalOrgId.PNG)
    
    To efficiently allow all members in the organization to read the S3 bucket, you need to use the `(*)`wildcard character as the principal. This will allow anonymous access on the bucket. Then, set a condition using the `PrincipalOrgId` key to limit the access to Organization members only.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Declare a `(*)`wildcard as the principal and set `PrincipalOrgId` as a condition.**
    
    The option that says: **Manually list all AWS member account as the principal** is incorrect because it will take a lot of time to list all 200 account numbers into the S3 bucket policy. This solution is not efficient.
    
    The option that says: **Define `PrincipalOrgId`as the principal** is incorrect because the principal key does not take `PrincipalOrgId` as a value.
    
    The option that says: **Declare the organization’s root account ID as the principal** is incorrect because this will just give access to the root account and will not give access to all AWS accounts in the organization.
    
- Declare the organization's root account ID as the principal.
- Define `PrincipalOrgId` as the principal.

A SysOps Administrator needs to assess the behavior of AWS resources and identify potential security issues in the cloud architecture. The Administrator is also required to produce a report on multiple activities that are occurring within the EC2 instances and gather data on these targets such as details of communication with other AWS services, use of secure channels, details of the running processes, network traffic among the running processes and many others.

Which of the following services will help the Administrator assess the EC2 instances?

- AWS Trusted Advisor
- AWS Systems Manager
- AWS Macie
- **Amazon Inspector**
    
    **Amazon Inspector** enables you to analyze the behavior of your AWS resources and helps you to identify potential security issues. You can create an assessment template and launch a security assessment run of this target.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/insp_def_assess_1.png)
    
    During the assessment run, the network, file system, and process activity within the specified target are monitored, and a wide set of activity and configuration data is collected. The collected data is correlated, analyzed, and compared to a set of security rules specified in the assessment template. A completed assessment run produces a list of findings – potential security problems of various severity.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Amazon Inspector.**
    
    **AWS Trusted Advisor** is incorrect because it only provides you with advice on how to optimize and secure your AWS environment, but it does not produce the details that you need for your report.
    
    **Amazon Macie** is incorrect because it is used to discover, classify, and protect sensitive data in AWS, such as personally identifiable information or intellectual property. However, it does not check for other security details, such as in network traffic and running processes.
    
    **AWS Systems Manager** is incorrect because it just provides you with a unified user interface with a centralized and consistent way to gather operational insights and carry out routine management tasks. It does not assess the security of your EC2 instances the way Inspector does.
    

A SysOps Administrator is setting up a CloudWatch Dashboard which should track the memory and swap usage of all Linux EC2 Instances in Amazon VPC. There would be an upcoming IT audit and this should be done as soon as possible.

Which of the following options should the Administrator do to fulfill this requirement?

- Register for a CloudWatch account to enable metrics monitoring.
- Enable basic monitoring on the EC2 instances.
- Enable detailed monitoring on the EC2 instances.
- **Install CloudWatch Agent on the EC2 Instances.**
    
    **Amazon CloudWatch** is a monitoring service for AWS cloud resources and the applications you run on AWS. You can use Amazon CloudWatch to collect and track metrics, collect and monitor log files, and set alarms. You can use Amazon CloudWatch to gain system-wide visibility into resource utilization, application performance, and operational health. You can use these insights to react and keep your application running smoothly.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/CW-Overview.png)
    
    You can use the CloudWatch agent to collect both system metrics and log files from Amazon EC2 instances and on-premises servers. The agent supports both Windows Server and Linux and enables you to select the metrics to be collected, including sub-resource metrics such as per-CPU core and memory usage.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Install CloudWatch Agent on the EC2 Instances.**
    
    The option that says: **Registering for a CloudWatch account to enable metrics monitoring** is incorrect because you don’t need to explicitly register for a CloudWatch account.
    
    The option that says: **Enable detailed monitoring on the EC2 instances** is incorrect because detailed monitoring just helps you to send metric data for your instance to CloudWatch in 1-minute periods.
    
    The option that says: **Enable basic monitoring on the EC2 instances** is incorrect because this just helps your EC2 instances send metric data to CloudWatch in 5-minute intervals.
    

A company has multiple AWS accounts that are consolidated using AWS Organizations. A Systems Engineer has been tasked to set up AWS IAM Identity Center to centrally manage access to all of the company’s AWS accounts and cloud applications. The Engineer has already created a directory in the master account using the AWS Directory Service. Full access has also been configured by the Engineer in Organizations.

Which of the following should the Engineer configure to complete the setup?

- Set up permission sets in Organizations. Associate the permission sets with AWS IAM Identity Center users and groups.
- For each member account, set up IAM roles that will be used by AWS IAM Identity Center. Associate the users with these IAM roles using AWS IAM Identity Center.
- Set up service control policies (SCPs) in Organizations. Associate the SCPs with Directory Service users and groups using the AWS Management Console.
- **Set up permission sets in AWS IAM Identity Center to give AWS access to IAM Identity Center Users.**
    
    AWS AWS IAM Identity Center manages access to all your AWS Organizations accounts and other business applications that support the Security Assertion Markup Language (SAML) 2.0 standard.
    
    You can configure your identity source in AWS IAM Identity Center to determine where your users and groups are stored. Once configured, you can then look up users or groups in your store to grant them single sign-on access to AWS accounts, cloud applications, or both.
    
    ![AWS IAM Identity Center](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/iam-identity-center-091323-1816.png)
    
    AWS IAM Identity Center automatically provides you with a store by default, which you can use to manage your users and groups within AWS IAM Identity Center. If you choose to store them in AWS IAM Identity Center, create your users and groups and assign their level of access to your AWS accounts and applications. Alternatively, you can choose to Connect to Your External Identity Provider using Microsoft Entra ID or connect to your Microsoft AD Directory using AWS Directory Service.
    
    A permission set is a collection of administrator-defined policies that AWS IAM Identity Center uses to determine a user’s effective permissions to access a given AWS account. Permission sets can contain either AWS-managed policies or custom policies that are stored in the AWS IAM Identity Center. Policies are essentially documents that act as containers for one or more permission statements. These statements represent individual access controls (allow or deny) for various tasks that determine what tasks users can or cannot perform within the AWS account.
    
    ![Permission sets](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/iam-identity-center-permission-set-091323-1809.png)
    
    Permission sets are stored in the AWS IAM Identity Center and are only used for AWS accounts. They are not used to manage access to cloud applications. Permission sets ultimately get created as IAM roles in a given AWS account, with trust policies that allow users to assume the role through the AWS IAM Identity Center.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Set up permission sets in AWS IAM Identity Center to give AWS access to IAM Identity Center Users.**
    
    The option that says: **For each member account, set up IAM roles that will be used by AWS IAM Identity Center. Associate the users with these IAM roles using AWS IAM Identity Center** is incorrect. In the AWS Identity Center, the level of access that users can have in an account is defined using permission sets. You cannot associate Identity Center users directly with IAM roles.
    
    The option that says: **Set up permission sets in Organizations. Associate the permission sets with AWS IAM Identity Center users and groups** is incorrect because you can only set up permission sets in AWS IAM Identity Center and not in AWS Organizations.
    
    The option that says: **Set up service control policies (SCPs) in Organizations. Associate the SCPs with Directory Service users and groups using the AWS Management Console** is incorrect. Service control policies (SCPs) are simply a feature of AWS Organizations designed to set the maximum permissions that identities (users and roles) in member accounts can have. They cannot be directly associated with Directory Service users.
    

A SysOps Administrator plans to set up a disaster and recovery plan in AWS. The requirement is to establish a durable, highly available backup and archiving strategy for the company-owned financial documents, which should be accessible immediately for 6 months. It is expected that there would be a compliance audit every 3 years. The Administrator needs to ensure that the files are still available during that period.

Which service should the Administrator use to fulfill these requirements in the most cost-effective manner?

- Set up a storage gateway to upload data in an Amazon S3 bucket. Configure lifecycle policies to archive the data to a Cold HDD Amazon EBS volume.
- Set up a Direct Connect connection to upload data to an Amazon S3 bucket. For archiving purposes, use IAM policies to move the data into Amazon Glacier.
- **Upload data to an Amazon S3 bucket. Use lifecycle policies to move the data to Amazon Glacier for archiving.**
    
    To manage your objects so that they are stored cost-effectively throughout their lifecycle, configure their Amazon S3 Lifecycle. An S3 Lifecycle configuration is a set of rules that define actions that Amazon S3 applies to a group of objects.
    
    You can use lifecycle policies to define actions that you want Amazon S3 to take during an object’s lifetime (for example, transition objects to another storage class, archive them, or delete them after a specified period of time).
    
    ![Amazon S3 Lifecycle](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/lifecycle-transitions-v2.png)
    
    You can add rules in a lifecycle configuration to tell Amazon S3 to transition objects to another Amazon S3 storage class. For example:
    
    – When you know objects are infrequently accessed, you might transition them to the STANDARD_IA storage class.
    
    – You might want to archive objects that you don’t need to access in real-time to the GLACIER storage class.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Upload data to an Amazon S3 bucket. Use lifecycle policies to move the data to Amazon Glacier for archiving.**
    
    The option that says: **Set up a storage gateway to upload data in an Amazon S3 bucket. Configure lifecycle policies to archive the data to a Cold HDD Amazon EBS volume** is incorrect. It’s not possible to transition data from Amazon S3 to Amazon EBS via lifecycle policies.
    
    The option that says: **Set up a Direct Connect connection to upload data to an Amazon S3 bucket. For archiving purposes, use IAM policies to move the data into Amazon Glacier** is incorrect. Direct Connect is not required to transfer data to Amazon S3 unless a private connection between an on-premises data center and AWS is specified. Also, IAM policies only set the permissions needed to carry out an action in AWS. S3 lifecycle policy is the correct answer.
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS DataSync to transfer the data to an Amazon S3 bucket for storage. Schedule periodic transfers to ensure backups are synchronized for compliance audits**is incorrect because DataSync is primarily used for large-scale data migration and synchronization rather than archival management. It does not automate lifecycle transitions to archival storage like Glacier. It is not ideal for a long-term and cost-effective solution.
    
- Use AWS DataSync to transfer the data to an Amazon S3 bucket for storage. Schedule periodic transfers to ensure backups are synchronized for compliance audits.

A SysOps Administrator launched a bastion host in Amazon VPC to only allow one IP address (143.20.2.137) to establish SSH and RDP connections to the EC2 instances that are deployed to a private subnet. The EC2 instances have an attached security group, with a Group ID of `sg-tut5d0jo`. To fortify the bastion host, the Administrator added the following inbound rules to its security group:

`Type: All TCPProtocol: TCPPort Range: 22Source: 143.20.2.137/32`

Type: All TCP

Protocol: TCP

Port Range: 3389

Source: 143.20.2.137/32

Type: All TCP

Protocol: TCP

Port Range: 22

Source: sg-tut5d0jo

However, after checking the VPC Flow Logs, the Administrator discovered that there are IP addresses other than the specified IP address that can access the bastion host. Which of the following is the root cause of this issue?

- The Network ACL allows access to all IP addresses which overrides the Security Group inbound rules
- **The allowed IP addresses in the security group of the EC2 instances `sg-tut5d0jo` are also able to connect to the bastion host**
    
    In this scenario, the security group of the bastion host has an inbound rule to allow the traffic that comes from the `sg-tut5d0jo` security group of the EC2 instances. This means that all traffic allowed by that security group can communicate with the bastion host.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/NM_diagram_061316_a.png)
    
    A security group acts as a virtual firewall for your instance to control inbound and outbound traffic. When you launch an instance in a VPC, you can assign up to five security groups to the instance. Security groups act at the instance level, not the subnet level. Therefore, each instance in a subnet in your VPC could be assigned to a different set of security groups. If you don’t specify a particular group at launch time, the instance is automatically assigned to the default security group for the VPC.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **The allowed IP addresses in the security group of the EC2 instances `sg-tut5d0jo` are also able to connect to the bastion host.**
    
    The option that says: **A netmask of `/32` allows all IP addresses in the `143.20.2.0` network, other than your IP (`143.20.2.137`)** is incorrect because a `/32` netmask is limited to one IP address and not the entire IP block.
    
    The option that says: **The port 3389 provides unrestrictive access to `143.20.2.137` and to other IP addresses** is incorrect because port 3389 is just the port used by Microsoft RDP (Remote Desktop Protocol).
    
    The option that says: **The Network ACL allows access to all IP addresses which overrides the Security Group inbound rules** is incorrect. Even though a Network ACL can allow or deny all IP addresses from accessing the bastion host, it does not override the rules set in the security group.
    
- A netmask of `/32` allows all IP addresses in the `143.20.2.0` network, other than your IP (`143.20.2.137`)
- The port 3389 provides unrestrictive access to `143.20.2.137` and to other IP addresses

A company launched a global cryptocurrency exchange portal which uses a total of 20 EC2 instances evenly deployed across 4 regions (5 instances per region). An Application Load Balancer has also been set up in each region to distribute the incoming traffic to the EC2 instances.

How can the SysOps Administrator set up the portal to maintain site availability if one of the 4 regions was to lose network connectivity for an extended period of time?

- 1. Set up a Route 53 Latency Based Routing Record Set that resolves to an Application Load Balancer in each region.2. Set an appropriate health check on each ELB.
- 1. Launch another Application Load Balancer to place in front of the EC2 instances across all regions.2. Set an appropriate health check on each ELB.
- 1. Set up a VPN Connection on each of the EC2 instances in each region.2. Configure a failover using BGP in the case of a region-wide connectivity outage.
- **1. Set up a Route 53 Latency Based Routing Record Set that resolves to the Application Load Balancers in each region.2. Set the Evaluate Target Health flag to true.**
    
    If your application is hosted in multiple AWS Regions, you can improve performance for your users by serving their requests from the AWS Region that provides the lowest latency.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/AWS-Route53-Latency.png)
    
    To use latency-based routing, you create latency records for your resources in multiple AWS Regions. When Route 53 receives a DNS query for your domain or subdomain (tutorialsdojo.com or portal.tutorialsdojo.com), it determines which AWS Regions you’ve created latency records for, determines which region gives the user the lowest latency, and then selects a latency record for that region. Route 53 responds with the value from the selected record, such as the IP address for a web server.
    
    Setting an appropriate “Health check on ELB” is not enough. Although this ensures that your ALB is only routing to healthy instances, Route 53 will not know if the ALB in that region has failed.
    
    You need to set the “Evaluate Target Health” flag to true on Route 53. This way, Route 53 will check both ALB entry to ensure that your ALBs are responding. Route 53 will then decide to which ALB it will direct the user. If one region goes down, Route 53 will know it via the “Evaluate Target Health” setting and will not redirect users to that region’s ALB.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is:
    
    **1. Set up a Route 53 Latency Based Routing Record Set that resolves to the Application Load Balancers in each region.**
    
    **2. Set the Evaluate Target Health flag to true.**
    
    The option that says: **1. Launch another Application Load Balancer to place in front of the EC2 instances across all regions. 2. Set an appropriate health check on each ELB** is incorrect. Although this solution provides redundancy, it does not address the issue of a region-wide connectivity outage. If one of the four regions loses network connectivity, the instances in that region would be unreachable and traffic would still be routed to the non-responsive ALB in that region.
    
    The option that says: **1. Set up a VPN Connection on each of the EC2 instances in each region. 2. Configure a failover using BGP in the case of a region-wide connectivity outage** is incorrect because it requires additional infrastructure and configuration. In addition, it does not address the issue of routing traffic to the non-responsive ALB in the disconnected region.
    
    The option that says: **1. Set up a Route 53 Latency Based Routing Record Set that resolves to an Application Load Balancer in each region. 2. Set an appropriate health check on each ELB** is incorrect because it also does not address the issue of routing traffic to a non-responsive ALB in a disconnected region.
    

One of the Systems Administrators in your team has created a VPC with a CIDR block of 10.0.0.0/16 using the VPC wizard. There is a requirement to implement a hybrid cloud architecture in which you have to connect the client’s data center to its VPC in AWS. You created a public subnet with a CIDR block of (10.0.0.0/24) and a VPN-only subnet with a CIDR block of (10.0.1.0/24) along with the VPN gateway (vgw-51898) to connect to the data center, which has a CIDR block of 172.16.0.0/12. To allow traffic to the internet from the VPN subnet, you also made an additional setup for a NAT instance (i-918273).

Which of the following options is not a valid entry for the main route table in this scenario?

- **Destination: 10.0.1.0/24 and Target: i-918273**
    
    The configuration for this scenario includes a virtual private cloud (VPC) with a public subnet and a private subnet, and a virtual private gateway to enable communication with your own network over an IPsec VPN tunnel. This is a recommended scenario if you want to extend your network into the cloud and also directly access the Internet from your VPC.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/aws_vpc_route_table_correct.png)
    
    This scenario enables you to run a multi-tiered application with a scalable web front end in a public subnet, and to house your data in a private subnet that is connected to your network by an IPsec VPN connection.
    
    In the main route table, the first entry is the default entry for local routing in the VPC; this entry enables the instances in the VPC to communicate with each other over IPv4. The second entry routes all other IPv4 subnet traffic from the private subnet to your network over the virtual private gateway (VPG).
    
    The only routing option which should have access to the Internet gateway should be the 0.0.0.0/0 address. There should be no VPN-only subnet that routes to a NAT instance.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Destination: 10.0.1.0/24 and Target: i-918273.**
    
    The option that says: **Destination: 0.0.0.0/0 and Target: i-918273** is incorrect because this will allow all traffic from the internet to NAT instance which is a valid route.
    
    The option that says: **Destination: 172.16.0.0/12 and Target: vgw-51898** is incorrect because this is a valid route that will allow traffic from the on-premises data center to the virtual gateway.
    
    The option that says: **Destination: 10.0.0.0/16 and Target: local** is incorrect because this is a default route used by resources to communicate within the VPC.
    
- Destination: 172.16.0.0/12 and Target: vgw-51898
- Destination: 0.0.0.0/0 and Target: i-918273
- Destination: 10.0.0.0/16 and Target: local

A company has numerous AWS accounts and is managed using AWS Organizations. The finance department has requested a monthly breakdown of cloud expenditures for each department. AWS Systems Manager OpsCenter is used as a centralized location to manage operational work items of the corporate AWS resources across AWS accounts.

What sequence of actions should the SysOps Administrator take in order to supply the required information? (Select TWO.)

- **In the AWS Organizations management account, go to the AWS Billing and Cost Management console and activate the cost allocation tag named "Department". Utilize a tag policy to enforce a "Department" tag on newly created resources.**
- Utilize AWS Systems Manager Fleet Manager to identify resources lacking tags in each account. Apply a tag named "Department" to any resources that are untagged.
- In each account, go to the AWS Billing and Cost Management console and activate the cost allocation tag named "Department".
- Configure an AWS Config rule across all accounts in the organization to mark resources without a "Department" tag as non-compliant.
- **Utilize the AWS Resource Groups Tag Editor to identify resources lacking tags in each account. Apply a tag named "Department" to any resources that are untagged.**
- **Evaluation**
    
    A tag is a label that users or AWS assigns to an AWS resource. Each tag consists of a key and a value. For each resource, each tag key must be unique, and each tag key can have only one value. Tags can be used to organize resources and **cost allocation tags** to track AWS costs on a detailed level.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/soa-c02-cost-allocation-tags.png)
    
    After **activating cost allocation tags**, AWS uses the cost allocation tags to organize resource costs on the cost allocation report to make it easier to categorize and track AWS costs. AWS provides two types of cost allocation tags, AWS-generated tags, and user-defined tags. Both types of tags must be activated separately before they can appear in Cost Explorer or on a cost allocation report.
    
    With **Tag Editor**, users can build a query to find resources in one or more AWS Regions that are available for tagging. Users can choose up to 20 individual resource types or build a query on All resource types. The query can include resources that already have tags or resources that have no tags.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– In the AWS Organizations management account, go to the AWS Billing and Cost Management console and activate the cost allocation tag named “Department”. Utilize a tag policy to enforce a “Department” tag on newly created resources.**
    
    **– Utilize the AWS Resource Groups Tag Editor to identify resources lacking tags in each account. Apply a tag named “Department” to any resources that are untagged.**
    
    The option that says: **Configure an AWS Config rule across all accounts in the organization to mark resources without a “Department” tag as non-compliant** is incorrect because AWS Config rules are primarily used to evaluate the compliance of resources against predefined configuration policies. It does not provide financial or billing information in AWS.
    
    The option that says: **Utilize AWS Systems Manager Fleet Manager to identify resources lacking tags in each account. Apply a tag named “Department” to any resources that are untagged** is incorrect because Fleet Manager is primarily used for providing performance insights and management capabilities for server fleets rather than resource tagging. It does not have the capability to identify untagged resources.
    
    The option that says: **In each account, go to the AWS Billing and Cost Management console and activate the cost allocation tag named “Department”** is incorrect because cost allocation tags are managed at the management account level rather than being handled separately for each account. The management account acts as the central point for managing cost allocation tags, allowing for consistent tagging practices across multiple AWS accounts.
    

A law firm uses an Amazon RDS DB instance to store data on their online inquiries and consultations. The firm is managing several Lambda functions that create, update, and delete records from the database. The DB credentials are hardcoded on the Lambda functions, which are used to connect to the database.

The SysOps administrator wants to improve the setup by ensuring that the DB credentials are not stored in plaintext and that the DB password is constantly rotated every 30 days.

What steps should the SysOps administrator perform to achieve the requirements in the MOST operationally efficient way?

- Utilize AWS Systems Manager Parameter Store to create secure strings for DB credentials. Build a new Lambda function and name it PasswordRotate. Schedule the new function to run every 30 days using Amazon EventBridge (Amazon CloudWatch) to generate a new password. Modify the environment variable on each Lambda function to access the secure strings stored in Parameter Store.
- **Use AWS Secrets Manager secrets instead of environment variables to store database credentials. While creating the secret, select the database so that the Secrets Manager will use a Lambda function to rotate the password for the database automatically. Specify the automatic rotation schedule to 30 days. Update all the Lambda functions to access the DB password from Secrets Manager.**
    
    You can use **environment variables** to adjust your function’s behavior without updating code. An environment variable is a pair of strings that are stored in a function’s version-specific configuration. The Lambda runtime makes environment variables available to your code and sets additional environment variables that contain information about the function and invocation request.
    
    To increase database security, we recommend that you use AWS Secrets Manager instead of environment variables to store database credentials.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/aws_lambda_rds_secrets.png)
    
    This approach will help you secure last-mile secrets and protect your backend databases. Long living credentials need to be managed and regularly rotated to keep access into critical systems secure, so it’s a security best practice to periodically reset your passwords. Manually changing the passwords would be cumbersome, but AWS Secrets Manager helps by managing and rotating the RDS database passwords.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/aws_secrets_manager_store_secret_for_rds_db.PNG)
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use AWS Secrets Manager secrets instead of environment variables to store database credentials. While creating the secret, select the database so that the Secrets Manager will use a Lambda function to rotate the password for the database automatically. Specify the automatic rotation schedule to 30 days. Modify all the Lambda functions to access the DB password from Secrets Manager.**
    
    The option that says: **Create an environment variable in each of the Lambda functions and store the DB password. Then, create a new Lambda function and name it PasswordRotate. Schedule the new function to run every 30 days using Amazon EventBridge (Amazon CloudWatch) to generate a new password and update the environment variable on each Lambda function** is incorrect. Using environment variables to store database passwords is bad practice. The better approach for storing database credentials like passwords is by storing the credentials using AWS Secret Manager.
    
    The option that says: **Encrypt the DB password using the AWS Key Management Service (KMS). Store the encrypted password as an environment variable in all Lambda functions. Then, grant each function the necessary permissions to access the KMS key so that the database password can be decrypted when needed. Build a Lambda function that can programmatically reset the DB password every 30 days** is incorrect. The AWS KMS service is only used to store cryptographic keys that are used to encrypt and decrypt data across your AWS workloads. It does not help you store database passwords, but it can be used to encrypt and decrypt secrets.
    
    The option that says: **Utilize AWS Systems Manager Parameter Store to create secure strings for DB credentials. Build a new Lambda function and name it PasswordRotate. Schedule the new function to run every 30 days using Amazon EventBridge (Amazon CloudWatch) to generate a new password. Modify the environment variable on each Lambda function to access the secure strings stored in Parameter Store** is incorrect because this will take time to set up. While this is doable, it is stated in the scenario that you need to find the most operationally efficient method to achieve the requirement. The AWS Secrets Manager automatically handles the rotation of DB passwords, which means you don’t need to create a new Lambda function just for the purpose of rotating passwords.
    
- Encrypt the DB password using the AWS Key Management Service (KMS). Store the encrypted password as an environment variable in all Lambda functions. Then, grant each function the necessary permissions to access the KMS key so that the database password can be decrypted when needed. Build a Lambda function that can programmatically reset the DB password every 30 days.
- Create an environment variable in each of the Lambda functions and store the DB password. Then, create a new Lambda function and name it PasswordRotate. Schedule the new function to run every 30 days using Amazon EventBridge (Amazon CloudWatch) to generate a new password and update the environment variable on each Lambda function.

A multinational investment bank plans to adopt a hybrid cloud architecture. To migrate their on-premises applications to AWS Cloud, the SysOps Administrator should prepare a couple of CloudFormation templates that will automatically provision the required resources.

Which section is required when designing a template?

- Outputs section
- Format Version section
- **Resources section**
    
    Templates include several major sections. The Resources section is the only required section. Some sections in a template can be in any order. However, as you build your template, it can be helpful to use the logical order shown in the following list because values in one section might refer to values from a previous section.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/5-parent-only.png)
    
    **Format Version (optional)**
    
    The AWS CloudFormation template version that the template conforms to. The template format version is not the same as the API or WSDL version. The template format version can change independently of the API and WSDL versions.
    
    **Description (optional)**
    
    A text string that describes the template. This section must always follow the template format version section.
    
    **Metadata (optional)**
    
    Objects that provide additional information about the template.
    
    **Parameters (optional)**
    
    Values to pass to your template at runtime (when you create or update a stack). You can refer to the parameters from the Resources and Outputs sections of the template.
    
    **Mappings (optional)**
    
    A mapping of keys and associated values that you can use to specify conditional parameter values, similar to a lookup table. You can match a key to a corresponding value by using the Fn::FindInMap intrinsic function in the Resources and Outputs section.
    
    **Conditions (optional)**
    
    Conditions that control whether certain resources are created or whether certain resource properties are assigned a value during stack creation or update. For example, you could conditionally create a resource that depends on whether the stack is for a production or test environment.
    
    **Transform (optional)**
    
    For serverless applications (also referred to as Lambda-based applications), specifies the version of the AWS Serverless Application Model (AWS SAM) to use. When you specify a transform, you can use AWS SAM syntax to declare resources in your template. The model defines the syntax that you can use and how it is processed. You can also use AWS::Include transforms to work with template snippets that are stored separately from the main AWS CloudFormation template. You can store your snippet files in an Amazon S3 bucket and then reuse the functions across multiple templates.
    
    **Resources (required)**
    
    Specifies the stack resources and their properties, such as an Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud instance or an Amazon Simple Storage Service bucket. You can refer to resources in the Resources and Outputs sections of the template.
    
    **Outputs (optional)**
    
    Describes the values that are returned whenever you view your stack’s properties. For example, you can declare an output for an S3 bucket name and then call the AWS CloudFormation describe-stacks AWS CLI command to view the name.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Resources section.**
    
    As stated above, the following options are incorrect:
    
    **– Outputs section**
    
    **– Format Version section**
    
    **– Parameters section**
    
- Parameters section

Large amounts of transactions are going through your banking system into your EC2 instances, causing degradation in its overall performance. After monitoring the event, you found out that the root cause of the problem is the data transferring to your EBS volumes. This is caused by a high volume of transactions exceeding your bandwidth capacity.

What would be a cost-effective solution to optimize EBS performance?

- Throttle the incoming traffic to enable your instances and EBS volumes to cope up with the volume of transactions.
- **If the instance type of your instance does not support EBS optimization, change your instance type to one that supports it.**
    
    An Amazon EBS–optimized instance uses an optimized configuration stack and provides additional, dedicated capacity for Amazon EBS I/O. This optimization provides the best performance for your EBS volumes by minimizing contention between Amazon EBS I/O and other traffic from your instance.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/amazon_aws_ebs_optimized.PNG)
    
    For applications with spiky Amazon EBS demand, you can right-size your instances based on your CPU and memory requirements and still meet your EBS-optimized instance performance requirements. This higher performance also enables you to speed up sections of your workflow dependent on EBS-optimized instance performance. Faster workflows result in quicker job completions and improved resource utilization. The burst capability ultimately enables you to reduce costs by right-sizing your instance and improving total resource usage.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **If the instance type of your instance does not support EBS optimization, change your instance type to one that supports it.**
    
    The option that says: **Increase the number of EBS volumes for your instances** is incorrect because you should try to enable EBS optimization first before increasing the number of EBS volumes that you have, and verify if EBS optimization will be enough to meet your performance requirements.
    
    The option that says: **Increase the number of instances supporting your system** is incorrect because you should run an instance that supports EBS optimization first to verify if EBS optimization can solve your performance requirements.
    
    The option that says: **Throttle the incoming traffic to enable your instances and EBS volumes to cope up with the volume of transactions** is incorrect since throttling your network might cause your transactions to pile up and affect your banking operations, which is counterproductive from what you are trying to solve.
    
- Increase the number of EBS volumes for your instances.
- Increase the number of instances supporting your system.

A tech company uses AWS Organizations to manage multiple AWS accounts. The SysOps administrator wants to ensure that all security groups attached to EC2 instances do not allow SSH traffic. She needs to aggregate the configuration data from different AWS accounts in multiple AWS regions.

What should the SysOps administrator do to achieve the requirements?

- Create an AWS Config aggregator resource for each resource in the organization. Create config rules on each AWS account to monitor security groups.
- **Create an AWS Config aggregator resource for all AWS accounts in the organization. Enable AWS Config and create a config rule on all accounts to monitor security groups.**
    
    An aggregator is an AWS Config resource type that collects AWS Config configuration and compliance data from the following:
    
    – Multiple accounts and multiple regions.
    
    – Single account and multiple regions.
    
    – An organization in AWS Organizations and all the accounts in that organization which have AWS Config enabled.
    
    Use an aggregator to view the resource configuration and compliance data recorded in AWS Config.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/amazon_aws_config_aggregator.png)
    
    On the **Aggregator** page, you can do the following:
    
    – Create an aggregator by specifying the source account IDs or organization and regions from which you want to aggregate data.
    
    – Edit and delete an aggregator.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Create an AWS Config aggregator resource for all AWS accounts in the organization. Enable AWS Config and create a config rule on all accounts to monitor security groups.**
    
    The option that says: **Create an AWS Config aggregator resource for each resource in the organization. Create config rules on each AWS account to monitor security groups** is incorrect because you can’t create an aggregator for each AWS resource. You only need to specify the source account ID and the region when creating an aggregator, not the specific resources that need to be monitored.
    
    The option that says: **Create a custom config rule to monitor changes to the security group in all EC2 instances provisioned in the management account** is incorrect. A custom config rule will not be enough to collect compliance status on multiple AWS accounts. You need to set up an aggregator.
    
    The option that says: **Create a managed config rule to monitor all security groups when they don’t comply with the rules** is incorrect because a managed config rule only monitors resources in a single AWS account. You need to collect compliance information about resources on other AWS accounts too, so this option does not meet the requirement.
    
- Create a custom config rule to monitor changes to the security group in all EC2 instances provisioned in the management account.
- Create a managed config rule to monitor all security groups when they don't comply with the rules.

A photo-sharing company is hosting an application on an Amazon EC2 instance that allows users to upload images. The application saves the images on an Amazon S3 bucket in `us-east-2`. Recently, users have been experiencing load time issues when uploading images into the application. The website is popular in countries away from `us-east-2`.

Which of the following should the SysOps administrator do to address the issue?

- **Enable S3 Transfer Acceleration on the bucket.**
    
    **Amazon S3 Transfer Acceleration** is a bucket-level feature that enables fast, easy, and secure transfers of files over long distances between your client and an S3 bucket. Transfer Acceleration takes advantage of the globally distributed edge locations in Amazon CloudFront. As the data arrives at an edge location, the data is routed to Amazon S3 over an optimized network path.
    
    ![Amazon S3 Transfer Acceleration](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/amazon_activate_s3_transfer_acceleration.PNG)
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Enable S3 Transfer Acceleration on the bucket.**
    
    The option that says: **Create accelerators and listeners using AWS Global Accelerator** is incorrect because the Global Accelerator service does not work with S3. It only supports endpoints like application load balancers, network load balancers, EC2 instances, or elastic IP addresses.
    
    The option that says: **Build S3 access points in AWS Regions closest to the users** is incorrect because S3 access points are only used for creating access policies that grant permissions to groups of users or applications on S3 buckets. It is not primarily used to improve upload speed on a bucket.
    
    The option that says: **Use the S3 `CreateMultipartUpload` API to improve upload performance for large images** is incorrect because while the multipart upload API allows for efficient parallel transfers of large files, it does not reduce latency caused by geographic distance from the S3 bucket. Transfer Acceleration is necessary to optimize global upload performance by utilizing edge locations.
    
- Create accelerators and listeners using AWS Global Accelerator.
- Build S3 access points in AWS Regions closest to the users.
- Use the S3 `CreateMultipartUpload`API to improve upload performance for large images.

A company is hosting a multi-tiered web application that consists of an e-commerce module as well as a blogging site that fetches data from a database. Some articles, which are static web pages, have lots of page hits and sometimes cause the application to behave slowly.

Which of the following can be used to alleviate the issue of slow loading times when many users are visiting the application for such pages, in the MOST cost-effective way?

- **Consider hosting the web pages using static web site hosting in S3**
    
    **Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3)** is an object storage service offering industry-leading scalability, data availability, security, and performance. Customers of all sizes and industries can store and protect any amount of data for virtually any use case, such as data lakes, cloud-native applications, and mobile apps. With cost-effective storage classes and easy-to-use management features, you can optimize costs, organize data, and configure fine-tuned access controls to meet specific business, organizational, and compliance requirements.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/Amazon-S3-StaticWebsiteHosting.png)
    
    You can utilize Amazon S3 to host static web pages. Static pages can also contain client-side scripts. By contrast, a dynamic website relies on server-side processing, including server-side scripts such as PHP, JSP, or ASP.NET. Amazon S3 does not support server-side scripting. In addition, you can also use a CloudFront distribution as a Content Delivery Network for your static contents.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Consider hosting the web pages using static web site hosting in S3.**
    
    The option that says: **Consider placing ElastiCache in front of the database** is incorrect because is ElastiCache is used for caching content being frequently fetched from a database.
    
    The option that says: **Consider creating a Read Replica of the database** is incorrect because Read Replicas are used for scaling read-heavy database workloads in RDS.
    
    The option that says: **Consider using the latency policy in Route 53** is incorrect since this is used for routing of traffic between multiple sites.
    
- Consider creating a Read Replica of the database
- Consider placing ElastiCache in front of the database
- Consider using the latency policy in Route 53

An office supply store chain currently runs a five-day sale on its e-commerce web application using burstable EC2 instances in an Auto Scaling Group (ASG) fronted by an Application Load Balancer (ALB). The ASG scales out when the CPU Utilization reaches 70%.

Users report slow load times even though the administrator observes a 20% CPU utilization across all instances.

Which solution can resolve the issue in the most cost-effective way?

- Artificially increase the CPU Utilization by decreasing the desired size of the ASG. The fewer instance you have in the ASG, the higher the CPU Utilization will be.
- Relocate the infrastructure to an AWS Region closer to the majority of the users.
- **Create an Amazon CloudFront distribution to decrease direct network traffic to the ALB.**
    
    **Amazon CloudFront** is a fast content delivery network (CDN) service that securely delivers data, videos, applications, and APIs to customers globally with low latency, high transfer speeds, all within a developer-friendly environment.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/aws_elb_asg_cloudfront_ec2.png)
    
    CloudFront works seamlessly with any AWS origin, such as Amazon S3, Amazon EC2, Elastic Load Balancing, or with any custom HTTP origin.
    
    In the scenario, users are experiencing slow load times despite low CPU utilization across the instances. This could indicate that the users are geographically far from the region where the instances are located. In such cases, latency caused by the distance between users and the instances can result in slower content delivery and degraded user experience. To address this issue, you can use Amazon CloudFront.
    
    Amazon CloudFront is a web service that speeds up the distribution of your content, such as .html, .css, .js, and image files, to your users. CloudFront delivers your content through a worldwide network of data centers called edge locations. When a user requests content that you’re serving with CloudFront, the request is routed to the edge location that provides the lowest latency (time delay), so that content is delivered with the best possible performance.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Create an Amazon CloudFront distribution to decrease direct network traffic to the ALB.**
    
    The option that says: **Enable unlimited mode for all the burstable instances in the ASG** is incorrect because this will only help you sustain high CPU utilization to handle more workloads but it will not improve the load time.
    
    The option that says: **Relocate the infrastructure to an AWS Region closer to the majority of the users** is incorrect. This approach may not be the most cost-effective solution, as it would require migrating the entire infrastructure. Additionally, it might not address the needs of users in other regions.
    
    The option that says: **Artificially increase the CPU Utilization by decreasing the desired size of the ASG. The fewer instance you have in the ASG, the higher the CPU Utilization will be** is incorrect because this will just cause more problems since the desired ASG size is reduced.
    
- Enable unlimited mode for all the burstable instances in the ASG.

A NodeJS application uses an S3 bucket to store user-generated data. The bucket policy should always be monitored to secure the data of the users and verify that the files are not publicly accessible over the Internet. You are also tasked to automate the assessment of your resource configurations and resource changes to ensure continuous compliance and self-governance across your AWS infrastructure.

Which of the following is the best approach to satisfy this requirement?

- Configure the bucket policy to deny all read and write actions from unauthorized users.
- **Use AWS Config.**
    
    **AWS Config** enables continuous monitoring of your AWS resources, making it simple to assess, audit, and record resource configurations and changes. AWS Config does this through the use of rules that define the desired configuration state of your AWS resources. AWS Config provides a number of AWS managed rules that address a wide range of security concerns such as checking if you encrypted your Amazon Elastic Block Store (Amazon EBS) volumes, tagged your resources appropriately, and enabled multi-factor authentication (MFA) for root accounts.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/AWS-Config-Overview.png)
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use AWS Config.**
    
    The options that say: **Configure the bucket policy to deny all read and write actions from unauthorized users** and **Configure the bucket policy of the S3 bucket using IAM to prevent any unauthorized access** are incorrect as simply configuring the S3 bucket policy is not enough to satisfy the given requirements. It does not provide a way to automate the assessment of your resource configurations and resource changes, unlike AWS Config.
    
    The option that says: **Enable MFA Delete and Versioning in the S3 bucket** is incorrect because versioning and MFA delete is mainly used to prevent accidental deletion of objects in the bucket. These features do not automate the assessment of your resource configurations and resource changes.
    
- Configure the bucket policy of the S3 bucket using IAM to prevent any unauthorized access.
- Enable MFA Delete and Versioning in the S3 bucket.

Due to the recent IT audit, a financial company was instructed to present the information on AWS environment compliance for Payment Card Industry (PCI) workloads. This is done to provide a better understanding of what specific areas of the PCI standards fall under the company’s responsibility in running their e-commerce website.

Which of the following AWS services should you use in order to provide the necessary information?

- AWS Trusted Advisor
- AWS Certificate Manager
- Amazon Macie
- **AWS Artifact**
    
    **AWS Artifact** is your go-to central resource for compliance-related information that matters to you. It provides on-demand access to AWS’ security and compliance reports and select online agreements. Reports available in AWS Artifact include our Service Organization Control (SOC) reports, Payment Card Industry (PCI) reports, and certifications from accreditation bodies across geographies and compliance verticals that validate the implementation and operating effectiveness of AWS security controls. Agreements available in AWS Artifact include the Business Associate Addendum (BAA) and the Nondisclosure Agreement (NDA).
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/2019-10-13_06-22-01-42f092eacae7258dd804c54ac64a6b7d.png)
    
    The Payment Card Industry Data Security Standard (PCI DSS) is a proprietary information security standard administered by the PCI Security Standards Council, which was founded by American Express, Discover Financial Services, JCB International, MasterCard Worldwide and Visa Inc.
    
    PCI DSS applies to *all* entities that store, process or transmit cardholder data (CHD) or sensitive authentication data (SAD), including merchants, processors, acquirers, issuers, and service providers. The PCI DSS is mandated by the card brands and administered by the Payment Card Industry Security Standards Council.
    
    The PCI DSS Attestation of Compliance (AOC) and Responsibility Summary is available to customers by using AWS Artifact, a self-service portal for on-demand access to AWS compliance reports.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is **AWS Artifact**.
    
    **Amazon Macie** is incorrect because it is just a security service that uses machine learning to automatically discover, classify, and protect sensitive data in AWS.
    
    **AWS Certificate Manager** is incorrect because this is a service that simply lets you easily provision, manage, and deploy public and private Secure Sockets Layer/Transport Layer Security (SSL/TLS) certificates for use with AWS services and your internal connected resources. This service does not store certifications or compliance-related documents.
    
    **AWS Trusted Advisor** is incorrect because this is just an online tool that provides you with real-time guidance to help you provision your resources following AWS best practices. It inspects your AWS environment and makes recommendations for saving money, improving system performance and reliability, or closing security gaps.
    

A tech startup plans to launch a data analytics application that uses a MongoDB database and an NGINX server. The SysOps Administrator is instructed to provision a highly available storage solution that provides more than 100,000 IOPS in 4kB random I/O reads for the database.

Which of the following options will meet this requirement?

- Enable the `AutoEnableIO` property on a General Purpose SSD (gp3) volume.
- Throughput Optimized HDD (st1) Amazon EBS Volumes
- Storage Optimized Instances
- **Provisioned IOPS SSD (io1) Amazon EBS Volumes**
    
    Provisioned IOPS SSD volumes utilize solid-state drives (SSDs) and are best suited for high-performance Amazon EBS storage volumes. They are intended for critical workloads with high IOPS and throughput requirements that demand low latency. These volumes deliver their specified IOPS performance 99.9% of the time. Specifically tailored for I/O-intensive workloads, such as database operations, Provisioned IOPS SSD (io1) volumes ensure consistent and reliable storage performance. When creating the volume, you can specify the desired IOPS rate, and Amazon EBS will maintain the provisioned performance 99.9% of the time. By combining multiple io1 volumes, you can achieve the necessary IOPS for your workload.
    
    ![EBS Volumes](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/Provisioned-IOPS-SSD-(io1)-081624.png)
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Provisioned IOPS SSD (io1) Amazon EBS Volumes.**
